has to satisfy the constraint that it is within distance 1 of

,

, and
\alpha
. When we choose

, we must ensure that it is distance 1 of
c
and

, hence we can always choose

such that it equals
t
. This is important as when we multiply the two terms in Equation (
313
), we can take summation
t
outside and take
\beta=t
, to get

\begin{split}&\sum_{c}\overline{W}^{2,1}\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}a_{1}&d\\
c&a_{3}\end{array}\Bigg|\ u\right)\overline{W}^{1,2}\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}a_{1}&c\\
a_{2}&a_{3}\end{array}\Bigg|\ -u\right)\\
&=\sum_{t,c}\overline{W}\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}a_{1}&\alpha\\
c&t\end{array}\Bigg|\ u-\gamma\right)\,\overline{W}\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha&d\\
t&a_{3}\end{array}\Bigg|\ u\right)\,\overline{W}^{1,2}\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}a_{1}&c\\
a_{2}&a_{3}\end{array}\Bigg|\ -u\right)\\
&=s^{1}_{0}(-u)^{-1}\sum_{t,\tilde{t},c}\overline{W}\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}a_{1}&\alpha\\
c&t\end{array}\Bigg|\ u-\gamma\right)\,\overline{W}\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha&d\\
t&a_{3}\end{array}\Bigg|\ u\right)\overline{W}\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\tilde{t}&t\\
a_{2}&a_{3}\end{array}\Bigg|\ -u\right)\,\overline{W}\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}a_{1}&c\\
\tilde{t}&t\end{array}\Bigg|\ -u+\gamma\right)\\
&\propto\sum_{t}\overline{W}\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha&d\\
t&a_{3}\end{array}\Bigg|\ u\right)\overline{W}\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha&t\\
a_{2}&a_{3}\end{array}\Bigg|\ -u\right)\propto\delta_{d,a_{2}}\end{split}

(314)

where we used unitarity for
\overline{W}^{1,1}
in the 3
rd

u-\gamma
and
-u+\gamma
with a proportionality factor. We again used unitarity in 4
th
line to finally get the result. The proportionality factor can be exactly calculated using Eq. (
8
) and it is

s_{0}^{1}(-u)^{-1}\frac{\sin(\gamma- u)\sin(\gamma+ u)}{\sin^{2}\gamma}\frac{\sin(2 \gamma- u)\sin u}{\sin^{2}\gamma}=-\frac{\sin(2 \gamma- u)\sin(\gamma+ u)\sin(\gamma- u)}{\sin^{3}\gamma}\,.

(315)

Using these weights, we can construct generalized transfer matrices,

\bra{a}\overline{T}^{p_{1},p_{2}}(u)\ket{b}=\prod_{j=1}^{2L}\overline{W}^{p_{1},p_{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}b_{j}&b_{j+1}\\
a_{j}&a_{j+1}\end{array}\Bigg|\ u\right)\,,

(316)

which satisfy the following relations

\overline{T}^{p_{1},p_{2}}_{0}\overline{T}^{p_{1},1}_{p_{2}}=f^{p_{1}}_{p_{2}}\overline{T}^{p_{1},p_{2}-1}_{0}+f^{p_{1}}_{p_{2}-1}\overline{T}^{p_{1},p_{2}+1}_{0}\,,

(317)

where

\overline{T}^{p_{1},p_{2}}_{k}=\overline{T}^{p_{1},p_{2}}(u+k\gamma)\,,\quad f^{p_{1}}_{p_{2}}=[s^{p_{1}}_{p_{2}}]^{2L}\,.

(318)

We can finally discuss how to relate the fused transfer matrices of this section, with the ones used in
section 6
. Starting from the form of Boltzmann weights for the two set of weights for the simple transfer matrix, and following the constructions, it is not hard to see that

\begin{split}T^{(1)}_{[0]}=T(u)&=U_{h}^{-1}\overline{T}^{1,1}(u)U_{h}\,,\\
U_{h}\ket{a_0, \ldots a_{2L - 1}}&=\left(\prod_{i=0}^{2L-1}\frac{(-1)^{\frac{a_{i}}{2}}}{\sqrt{\theta_{a_{i}}}}\right)\ket{a_0, \ldots a_{2L - 1}}\,.\end{split}

(319)

For the higher fusion transfer matrices, using the two fusions in Eq. (
74
) and (
317
), we finally find

T^{(k)}_{[\frac{k-1}{2}]}=U_{h}^{-1}\overline{T}^{1,k}(u)U_{h}\,.

(320)

Appendix F
Technical issues related with the spectral parameters

F.1
Defect identifications for the defect Hamiltonian and transfer matrices in the direct channel

We discuss in this appendix the potential difference between the low-energy limits of the defect Hamiltonian and the defect transfer matrix as the bulk spectral parameter
u
is varied for a fixed
\tilde{u}
(or
v_{B}
is varied for fixed
v_{I}
in other notations).

Let us consider the largest eigenvalue of the row to row transfer matrix in the direct channel as function of the bulk parameter
v_{B}
and the defect parameter
v_{I}
. The Hamiltonian derived from this transfer matrix depends only on
v_{I}
, and the ordering of eigen-energies is fixed once this parameter is set. Meanwhile, one can easily see that, as
v_{B}
is varied, the ordering of the transfer matrix eigenvalues (recall that, by integrability, the eigenvectors do not depend on
v_{B}
) may change, so that the correspondence between largest eigenvalues and low-lying energies is affected. This is clear in the context of bulk transitions, e.g. ferromagnetic vs. antiferromagnetic transition, where by varying
v_{B}
we may find that the dominant eigenvalue of the transfer matrix is associated with an eigenstate which is the ground-state of
-H
instead of
H
. In this paper, however, we keep
|\imaginary v_{B}|<\gamma/2
(the Hamiltonian is obtained by sending
iv_{B}
to
-{\gamma\over 2}^{+}
) so that the underlying bulk theory is still given by the minimal model of A type.

We start with a case discussed in figure
58
-a), where we took a finite system of size
2L=8
for the
A_{4}
model, and an impurity parameter
v_{I}=-0.9~i\gamma
.

We consider first the transfer-matrix eigenvalues. The dashed blue line is obtained by taking the largest eigenvalue at the isotropic point
v_{B}=0
and then continuing analytically to other values of
v_{B}
in the ferromagnetic regime. Instead, the red line is obtained by taking the largest eigenvalue at
-iv_{B}
to
-{\gamma\over 2}^{+}
, and performing a similar continuation. Observe the presence of a crossing. By studying the same phenomenon in higher sizes (note that the position of the crossing depends on
L
), we can ascertain that, to the right of the crossing, the state corresponding to the blue curve does not belong to the scaling limit defined by low-energy excitations over the state from the red curve, and conversely to the left of the crossing.

As for the Hamiltonian, it is not hermitian (in finite system size), and eigen-energies may have imaginary parts, that tend to vanish as the size is increased. On the figure, we wrote down in blue and red the eigen-energies obtained by taking the logarithmic derivatives (at
iv_{B}
to
-{\gamma\over 2}^{+}
) of the transfer matrix eigenvalues represented on the blue and red curves.

We see on this figure
58
-a) that the dominant eigenvalue corresponds to the ground-state of the Hamiltonian (the lowest eigenenergy) only in the right most portion. When
-iv_{B}
is sufficiently negative however, this is not true any longer.

Now, extending the result for the Hamiltonian, we checked that the blue line corresponds, for the transfer matrix also, to the ground-state of the system with identity defect in the whole region to the right of the crossing. The red line, which, recall, does not belong to the scaling limit to the right of the crossing, corresponds, on the other hand, to the ground-state for
(2,1)
defect on the left of the crossing.

Hence, for the chosen value of
v_{I}
, we see that, depending on the bulk parameter
v_{B}
, the transfer matrix provides a realization of either of these two defects. In the vicinity of
v_{B}=0
- that is, the isotropic point - we get the identity defect, just like we did for the Hamiltonian. This observation extends to all other cases considered in the bulk of this paper.

(a)

(b)

(c)

Figure 58
:
Distinction between phase transitions for the Hamiltonian and transfer matrix. For fixed values of the defect parameter, the effective low-energy Hamiltonian description does not always correspond to the dominant eigenvalues of the transfer matrix, as the latter depend also on the bulk parameter. But it does at the isotropic point
v_{B}=0
for sufficiently large system-size. Here the data is for the
A_{4}
model so
\gamma={\pi\over 5}
.

In figure
58
-b), we set
v_{I}=-1.1~i\gamma
instead. In this case, the blue and red lines play reversed roles, but once again the identification of the defect carried out for the Hamiltonian applies in the vicinity of the isotropic point
v_{B}=0
(in this case, we get the
(2,1)
defect).

Results in figures a) and b) are general: Hamiltonian and transfer matrix in the vicinity of the isotropic point
v_{B}=0
give rise, in the scaling limit, to the same defect for the same
v_{I}
. The level crossing between transfer-matrix eigenvalues may occur at either smaller or larger values of
-iv_{B}
, depending of the value of
v_{I}
itself. At exact point
v_{I}=\pm i\gamma
, the Hamiltonian becomes singular (see equations (
40
-
41
)), which marks its phase transition. This is also a phase transition point for the transfer-matrix at the isotropic point, figure
58
-c). However, to see this unambiguously one has to take the large system-size limit to clean eigenvalues from finite-size contributions beyond
O(1)
.

It may sound a bit strange that while we discuss defect phase transitions, it is the bulk parameter that is being varied. We repeat that, as far as the Hamiltonian is concerned,
v_{B}
plays no role as long as it leads to the correct bulk theory. As for the transfer matrices, equations (
124
) and (
129
) for the identity defect show that the
O(1)
correction contains a non-universal term
e^{(1)}(v_{B}+v_{I})
that depends only on
v_{B}+v_{I}
, and thus allows the tradeoff between the two parameters to assess the phase transition. The competing states also have a non-universal contribution of the same type, say
e^{(p-2)}(v_{B}+v_{I}\pm\pi\mathrm{i}/2)
, where the correct sign is chosen so that
|\imaginary(v_{B}+v_{I})\pm\pi/2|<p\gamma/2
. It is the crossover between these
O(1)
corrections that triggers the transitions. In fact, the transition extends from points
v_{B}+v_{I}=\pm\mathrm{i}\gamma
to lines in the complex plane defined by the condition

\real(~e^{(p-2)}(v_{B}+v_{I}\pm\pi\mathrm{i}/2)-e^{(1)}(v_{B}+v_{I})~)=0,

(321)

where in figure
58
-c) we denote
\Lambda_{\text{def}}=\exp(e^{(1)}(\vbu+\vd)),~\exp(e^{(p-2)}(\vbu+\vd\pm\pi\im/2))
, corresponding respectively to the dashed blue and red lines.

While transition lines for the transfer matrices are
v_{B}
dependent, only at
v_{B}=0
do they match the corresponding transitions of the Hamiltonian, leading to parallel lines to the real
v_{I}
-axis:
|\imaginary v_{I}|=\gamma
. This is reminiscent of the distortion occurring in going from the lattice to the continuum, for the direction of propagation. Only at
v_{B}=0
the statistical model reproduces the partition function with a time-evolution as given by
H^{k,k+1}(\tilde{u})
.

To conclude, Hamiltonian and transfer-matrix results for the defects coincide when the latter is at the isotropic point. For other values of
v_{B}
, the correspondence of defects - albeit similar in nature - is more complicated, and doesn’t seem worth pursuing here.

F.2
On the Hermiticity of the defect Hamiltonians

In this subsection, we will first discuss what happens for values of the defect spectral parameter other than
0,\pm\frac{\pi}{2}
, and
\pm{\rm i}\infty
- where, as mentioned, lattice defect Hamiltonians are not hermitian, but, if
\tilde{u}
belongs to the proper strip, are still expected to realize the
(1,1)
,
(2,1)
, and
(1,2)
defect Hamiltonians in the continuum limit.

To be more specific, let us consider the Hamiltonian in Eq. (
40
)

H^{k,k+1}(\tilde{u})=H+\frac{1}{\sin\gamma}\left(f(\tilde{u})e_{k}e_{k+1}+f(-\tilde{u})e_{k+1}e_{k}\right)\,.

(322)

When
\tilde{u}
is purely imaginary,
f(\tilde{u})^{\star}=f(-\tilde{u})
, and
H^{k,k+1}(\tilde{u})
is Hermitian (this situation was studied in
tavares2024
in the context of the flow between
(1,2)
and
(1,1)
defects). The same holds when
\real(\tilde{u})=\pm\frac{\pi}{2}
(this situation was studied in
tavares2024
in the context of the flow between
(1,2)
and
(2,1)
defects).
But when
\tilde{u}
is real and generic (that is, except for
\tilde{u}=0,\pm\frac{\pi}{2}
, where
f(\tilde{u})=f(-\tilde{u})=0
)
f(\tilde{u})^{\star}\neq f(-\tilde{u})
, and
H^{k,k+1}(\tilde{u})
is not hermitian.

In these non-hermitian cases, it turns out that the eigen-energies are generically complex. We can then arrange these eigen-energies in ascending order of their real part. We observe in our problem that, either the low-lying eigen-energies are real, or, if they are complex their phases are small and tend to zero with increasing system size. This is illustrated with some examples in figure
59
and
60
for the A
4
and A
5
RSOS models.

Figure 59
:
For each impurity parameter, we find a low-lying level for which the eigen-energy is complex. We extract its phase and plot it with system size for A
4
RSOS.

Figure 60
:
This figure is same as figure
59
but for A
5
RSOS.

We can more generally form some simple quantities to evaluate the “non-hermiticity” of the Hamiltonians, and see that it decreases with increasing system size. See the figures
61
and
62
for some evidences for this

Figure 61
:
In this figure we show how with increasing system size the normalized operator:
\frac{\lvert H^{k,k+1}(\tilde{u})-H^{k,k+1}(\tilde{u})^{\dagger}\rvert}{\lvert H^{k,k+1}(\tilde{u})+H^{k,k+1}(\tilde{u})^{\dagger}\rvert}
decreases for different values of spectral parameter between
0
and
\frac{\pi}{2}
for A
4
RSOS model.

Figure 62
:
This figure is same as figure
61
but for A
5
RSOS.

Next, it is interesting to see what happens for the identification of the defect as we move
v_{I}
through the strips we identified with the Bethe-ansatz.

Figure 63
:
Here we study the ground state of Hamiltonian as the impurity parameter
v_{I}
is varied in
[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
and calculate the corresponding conformal dimension from the real part of the eigen-energy. In this figure we study the A
4
model and systems of size 14 to 20.

but for A

Appendix G
Y
operator and D operators in the Ising model

In this appendix, we show the equivalence between our
Y
operator for A
3
RSOS model, the D operator of
Seiberg:2024gek
(which implements KW duality in the Ising model) and the symmetry operator of
Gils_2009
;
Buican:2017rxc
(which was defined using
F-
symbol of the su
(2)_{3}
category).

The D operator was defined in
Seiberg:2024gek
as

\text{D}=\text{e}^{2\pi i\frac{R}{8}}\frac{1+\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{1-i\sigma^{x}_{R}}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{1-i\sigma^{z}_{R}\sigma^{z}_{R-1}}{\sqrt{2}}\cdots\frac{1-i\sigma^{z}_{2}\sigma^{z}_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{1-i\sigma^{x}_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}\,,

(323)

which by using
\varepsilon=\prod_{i=1}^{R}\sigma^{x}_{i}
can also be written as

\text{D}=\text{D}_{1}+\text{D}_{2}\,,

(324)

where

\text{D}_{1}=\frac{\text{e}^{2\pi i\frac{R}{8}}}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{1-i\sigma^{x}_{R}}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{1-i\sigma^{z}_{R}\sigma^{z}_{R-1}}{\sqrt{2}}\cdots\frac{1-i\sigma^{z}_{2}\sigma^{z}_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{1-i\sigma^{x}_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}\,,

(325)

and

\text{D}_{2}=\frac{\text{e}^{2\pi i\frac{R}{8}}}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{\sigma^{x}_{R}-i}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{1+i\sigma^{z}_{R}\sigma^{z}_{R-1}}{\sqrt{2}}\cdots\frac{1+i\sigma^{z}_{2}\sigma^{z}_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{\sigma^{x}_{1}-i}{\sqrt{2}}\,.

(326)

Now using the form of TL generators

e_{2i-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(1+\sigma_{i}^{x}\right)\,,\quad e_{2i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(1+\sigma_{i}^{z}\sigma_{i}^{z+1}\right)\,,

(327)

we can write

\begin{split}g_{2j-1}=\text{e}^{\frac{3\text{i}\pi}{8}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\sigma^{x}_{j}-i\right)\,,\quad g_{2j}=\text{e}^{\frac{-\text{i}\pi}{8}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(1+i\sigma^{z}_{j}\sigma^{z}_{j+1}\right)\,,\\
g_{2j-1}^{-1}=\text{e}^{\frac{\text{i}\pi}{8}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(1-i\sigma^{x}_{j}\right)\,,\quad g_{2j}^{-1}=\text{e}^{\frac{\text{i}\pi}{8}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(1-i\sigma^{z}_{j}\sigma^{z}_{j+1}\right)\,.\end{split}

(328)

44
44

44

One has to be careful about the branch cuts here, must get
(-q)^{1/2}=\text{e}^{\frac{-3\pi i}{8}}
and
(-q)^{-1/2}=\text{e}^{\frac{3\pi i}{8}}

Hence, we have

\text{D}_{1}=\frac{\text{e}^{\frac{-\pi i}{8}}}{\sqrt{2}}g_{2R-1}g_{2R-2}\ldots g_{1}\,,

(329)

and

\text{D}_{2}=\frac{\text{e}^{\frac{\pi i}{8}}}{\sqrt{2}}g_{2R-1}^{-1}g_{2R-2}^{-1}\ldots g_{1}^{-1}\,.

(330)

Note that
\tau^{2}=T
, where
T
is the lattice translation operator for Ising. Further, we can write

\begin{split}Y&=(-q)^{-1/2}\,\,g^{-1}_{1}g^{-1}_{2}\ldots g_{2R-1}^{-1}\tau^{-1}+(-q)^{1/2}\,\tau\,g_{2R-1}g_{2R-2}\ldots g_{1}\,,\\
&=\text{e}^{\frac{3\pi i}{8}}g^{-1}_{2R}g^{-1}_{1}\ldots g_{2R-2}^{-1}\tau^{-1}+\text{e}^{\frac{-3\pi i}{8}}\tau\,g_{2R-2}g_{2R-3}\ldots g_{1}g_{2R}\,,\\
&=\text{e}^{\frac{3\pi i}{8}}g^{-1}_{2L}g^{-1}_{1}\ldots g_{2L-2}^{-1}\tau^{-1}+\text{e}^{\frac{-3\pi i}{8}}g_{2L-1}g_{2L-2}\ldots g_{1}\tau\,.\end{split}

(331)

Similarly, we can write
\overline{Y}
as

\overline{Y}=\text{e}^{\frac{3\pi i}{8}}g_{2L-1}^{-1}g_{2L-2}^{-1}\ldots g_{1}^{-1}\tau+\text{e}^{\frac{-3\pi i}{8}}g_{2L}g_{1}\ldots g_{2L-2}\tau^{-1}\,.

(332)

For diagonal models we know that
Y=\overline{Y}
, hence we can write

\begin{split}&Y\tau^{-1}=\frac{\text{e}^{i\pi/4}}{\sqrt{2}}Y\tau^{-1}+\frac{\text{e}^{-i\pi/4}}{\sqrt{2}}\overline{Y}\tau^{-1}\\
&\implies Y\tau^{-1}=\frac{\text{e}^{\frac{5\pi i}{8}}}{\sqrt{2}}g_{2L}^{-1}g_{1}^{-1}\ldots g_{2L-2}^{-1}T^{-1}+\frac{\text{e}^{\frac{-\pi i}{8}}}{\sqrt{2}}g_{2L-1}g_{2L-2}\ldots g_{1}\\
&+\frac{\text{e}^{\frac{\pi i}{8}}}{\sqrt{2}}g_{2L-1}^{-1}g_{2L-2}^{-1}\ldots g_{1}^{-1}+\frac{\text{e}^{\frac{-5\pi i}{8}}}{\sqrt{2}}g_{2L}g_{1}\ldots g_{2L-2}T^{-1}\,.\end{split}

(333)

Now, as

q=\left(g_{2L}g_{1}\ldots g_{2L-2}\right)\left(g_{2L-2}g_{2L-3}\ldots g_{1}g_{2L}\right)\,,

(334)

the first and fourth term in Equation (
333
) cancel out and we have

Y\tau^{-1}=\frac{\text{e}^{\frac{-\pi i}{8}}}{\sqrt{2}}g_{2L-1}g_{2L-2}\ldots g_{1}+\frac{\text{e}^{\frac{\pi i}{8}}}{\sqrt{2}}g_{2L-1}^{-1}g_{2L-2}^{-1}\ldots g_{1}^{-1}=\text{D}_{1}+\text{D}_{2}=\text{D}\,.

(335)

To prove the relation in Equation (
334
) above, one just has to write the braid operators in terms of Pauli operators. Note, this relation is only valid for TFI, and not a general RSOS model.

Appendix I
Correspondence between the translation and line operator

Let
H^{k}_{{\cal D}}
be a defect Hamiltonian and
T^{k}_{{\cal D}}
be a (unitary) local translation operator for this defect Hamiltonian, i.e.

T^{k}_{{\cal D}}H^{(k,k+1)}_{{\cal D}}\left(T^{k}_{{\cal D}}\right)^{-1}=H^{(k-1,k)}_{{\cal D}}\,,

(336)

and therefore
\tau\,T^{k}_{{\cal D}}
commutes with the defect Hamiltonian and can be used to calculate the momentum eigenvalue of eigenstates. Although there is no general prescription, in certain cases using the translation operator one can construct the line operator in the crossed channel corresponding to
{\cal D}
, i.e.
\widehat{{\cal D}}^{(\rm latt)}
, for example see
Seifnashri:2023dpa
;
Sinha:2023hum
for invertible defects and
Seiberg:2024gek
for non-invertible defects.

Let us consider first the case of one impurity Hamiltonians ( Eq. (
39
)), for which the momentum and local translation operators are given in Eq. (
43
) and (
45
) respectively. Setting
k=1
, Eq. (
46
) implies

R_{1}(\tilde{u})\,H^{(1,2)}(\tilde{u})\,R_{1}(\tilde{u})^{-1}=H^{(0,1)}(\tilde{u})\,.

(337)

Successive application of the translation operator gives us

\begin{split}R_{1}(\tilde{u})R_{2}(\tilde{u})&\ldots R_{2\mathrm{R}-1}(\tilde{u})\,H^{(2\mathrm{R}-1,2\mathrm{R})}(\tilde{u})\,R_{2\mathrm{R}-1}(\tilde{u})^{-1}\ldots R_{2}(\tilde{u})^{-1}R_{1}(\tilde{u})^{-1}=H^{(2\mathrm{R}-1,0)}(\tilde{u})\,,\\
\implies&\left[H^{(0,1)}(\tilde{u}),R_{1}(\tilde{u})R_{2}(\tilde{u})\ldots R_{2\mathrm{R}-1}(\tilde{u})\tau^{-1}\right]=0\,,\\
\implies&\left[H^{(0,1)}(\tilde{u}),T_{A}(\gamma-\tilde{u})\right]=0\,,\end{split}

(338)

where the first implication follows from the fact that
\tau^{-1}H^{(0,1)}(\tilde{u})\tau=H^{(2\mathrm{R}-1,2\mathrm{R})}
, while the second implication follows as
\tilde{R}_{j}(\gamma-\tilde{u})=R_{j}(\tilde{u})
. Recall,
T_{A}
was defined in Eq. (
19
). Further, from the first line in Eq. (
337
) we also obtain

\begin{split}&\left[H^{(0,1)}(\tilde{u}),\tau R_{2\mathrm{R}-1}(\tilde{u})^{-1}\ldots R_{2}(\tilde{u})^{-1}R_{1}(\tilde{u})^{-1}\right]=0\,,\\
\implies&\left[H^{(0,1)}(\tilde{u}),\tau R_{2\mathrm{R}-1}(-\tilde{u})\ldots R_{2}(-\tilde{u})R_{1}(-\tilde{u})\right]=0\,,\\
\implies&\left[H^{(0,1)}(\tilde{u}),T_{B}(-\tilde{u})\right]=0\,,\end{split}

(339)

where the first implication above follows from unitarity of
R
matrix and
T_{B}
was also defined in Eq. (
19
).

Further, note that the defect shift operator for the defect Hamiltonian is related to these two line operators,
T_{A}(\gamma-\tilde{u})
and
T_{B}(-\tilde{u})
as follows

\begin{split}&\left(\tau R_{0}(\tilde{u})\right)^{2\mathrm{R}-1}=R_{1}(\tilde{u})R_{2}(\tilde{u})\ldots R_{2\mathrm{R}-1}(\tilde{u})\tau^{-1}\propto T_{A}(\gamma-\tilde{u})\,,\\
&\left(R_{0}(\tilde{u})^{-1}\tau^{-1}\right)^{2\mathrm{R}-1}=\tau R_{2\mathrm{R}-1}(\tilde{u})^{-1}\ldots R_{1}(\tilde{u})^{-1}\propto T_{B}(-\tilde{u})\,.\end{split}

(340)

Note,
R_{0}(\tilde{u})^{-1}\tau^{-1}
is the inverse of the usual defect shift operator,
\tau R_{0}(\tilde{u})
. Hence if we knew the eigenvalues of
T_{A}(\gamma-\tilde{u})
or
T_{B}(-\tilde{u})
, using the above equation we could determine the eigenvalues of
\tau R_{0}(\tilde{u})
and hence the momentum eigenvalues. The eigenvalues of
T_{A}(\gamma-\tilde{u})
and
T_{B}(-\tilde{u})
are not known generally, but when
\tilde{u}=0

or
\pm{\rm i}\infty
, there are certain simplifications. When
\tilde{u}=0
, i.e. the (1,1) defect case, we get from Eq. (
340
) that
\tau^{2\mathrm{R}}=\mathbb{1}
, which implies that the eigenvalues of the unitary operator
\tau
are

{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\frac{2\pi n}{2\mathrm{R}}}\,,\quad{\rm where}\,\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(341)

As
\tau
is the shift operator for the (1,1) defect Hamiltonian, by taking log of eigenvalues in Eq. (
345
), we get
{\rm i}\frac{2\pi(h-\bar{h})}{2\mathrm{R}}
, where
h
and
\bar{h}
are the conformal dimensions that appear in the partition function. Note, the fact that all states in A-type minimal model CFTs have
h-\bar{h}\in\mathbb{Z}
, lines up with the observation that
n\in\mathbb{Z}
in Eq. (
341
).

We will next discuss the case
\tilde{u}={\rm i}\infty
, the
\tilde{u}=-{\rm i}\infty
being very similar.

When
\tilde{u}={\rm i}\infty
, the defect Hamiltonian corresponds to the
(1,2)
defect. The defect shift operator in that case is
\tau g_{0}
, which is not exactly
\tau\lim_{\tilde{u}\to{\rm i}\infty}R_{0}(\tilde{u})
but proportional to it.
45
45

45

\tau\lim_{\tilde{u}\to{\rm i}\infty}R_{0}(\tilde{u})
diverges, therefore one has to rescale it to get the correct momentum eigenvalues.
The line operator corresponding to it are

\begin{split}(\tau g_{0})^{2\mathrm{R}-1}&=\quad g_{1}\ldots g_{2\mathrm{R}-1}\,\tau^{-1}\propto\lim_{\tilde{u}\to{\rm i}\infty}T_{A}(\gamma-\tilde{u})\,,\\
(g_{0}^{-1}\tau^{-1})^{2\mathrm{R}-1}&=\quad\tau g_{2\mathrm{R}-1}\ldots g_{1}^{-1}\propto\lim_{\tilde{u}\to{\rm i}\infty}T_{B}(-\tilde{u})\,.\\
\end{split}

(342)

Recall that in the limit of spectral parameter going to
-{\rm i}\infty
, the transfer matrix becomes the
\overline{Y}
operator, which we now write as

\overline{Y}=\overline{Y}_{A}+\overline{Y}_{B}
, where

\overline{Y}_{A}=(-q)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,\tau\,g^{-1}_{2\mathrm{R}-1}\ldots g_{1}^{-1}\,,\quad\overline{Y}_{B}=(-q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,g_{1}\ldots g_{2\mathrm{R}-1}\,\tau^{-1}\,.

(343)

The line operator
\overline{Y}
is the same as
Y
. Now, one can see easily that
\overline{Y}_{A}^{-1}=\overline{Y}_{B}
, further as
g_{i}^{\dagger}=g_{i}^{-1}
,
\overline{Y}_{A}^{\dagger}=\overline{Y}_{B}
. Hence,
\overline{Y}_{A}
and
\overline{Y}_{B}
are unitary operators, which are inverses of each other, therefore their eigenvalues are phases, say
{\rm e}^{-{\rm i}\theta}
and
{\rm e}^{{\rm i}\theta}
. Further, as they are inverses of each other, their eigenvectors are the same and therefore the eigenvalue of
\overline{Y}
is
2\cos\theta
. Now, we know the eigenvalues of
\overline{Y}
in A
p
RSOS model are given by

\begin{split}&q^{s}+q^{-s}=2\cos\left(\frac{s\pi}{p+1}\right)\,,\quad{\rm where}\,\,1\leq s\leq p\\
\implies&\theta=2n\pi\pm\frac{s\pi}{p+1}\,,\quad{\rm where}\,\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\,.\end{split}

(344)

As
(-q)^{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau g_{0})^{2\mathrm{R}-1}=\overline{Y}_{B}
, the eigenvalue of
\tau g_{0}
is

\begin{split}\left(\exp(\rm i \theta)(-q)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mathrm{R}-1}}&=\left(\exp\left({\rm i}\theta+{\rm i}\frac{\gamma-\pi}{2}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mathrm{R}-1}}\\
&=\exp\left({\rm i}\frac{2\pi}{2\mathrm{R}-1}\left(\left(n-\frac{1}{4}\right)\pm\frac{\left(s\pm\frac{1}{2}\right)}{2(p+1)}\right)\right)\\
\end{split}

(345)

\tau g_{0}
is the defect shift operator for the
(1,2)
defect Hamiltonian in Eq. (
51
), using the log of eigenvalues in Eq. (
345
), we get value of
h-\bar{h}
, where
h
and
\bar{h}
are the conformal dimension appearing in the twisted partition function. Note, from Eq. (
345
) we see that the spin chain must be treated to be of size
2\mathrm{R}-1
, instead of
2\mathrm{R}
like in the (1,1) defect case, to get accurate momentum scaling, as we have discussed in section
8
. It can be checked by appropriately selecting
n
and
s
, that we can recover the states which appear in partition function twisted by the
(1,2)
field.

We note that the (2,1) defect case is more complex: we can obtain it by setting
\tilde{u}={\pm\pi/2}
, but we do not have any analytical way to obtain the eigenvalue of
T_{A}(\gamma-\tilde{u})
or
T_{B}(-\tilde{u})
for these values of
\tilde{u}
.

Now, let us consider the
(1,3)
defect Hamiltonian
H^{0,1,2}_{{\cal D}_{(1,2)}{\cal D}_{(1,2)}}
, the defect shift operator for this Hamiltonian is given by
\tau g_{1}g_{0}
. Let us study

\left(\tau g_{1}g_{0}\right)^{2\mathrm{R}-2}=\left(g_{2}g_{3}\ldots g_{2\mathrm{R}-1}\right)\left(g_{1}g_{2}\ldots g_{2\mathrm{R}-2}\right)\tau^{-2}\,.

(346)

Recall, to obtain the states of
(1,3)
we have to use the Jones-Wenzl projector,
P_{1}^{(1)}
.
P_{1}^{(1)}
acts as identity on low-lying eigenstates of this Hamiltonian, which flow to states of
(1,3)
defect Hilbert space, therefore
e_{1}
must act as 0. Hence, if
\ket{\psi}
lies in the projected Hilbert space then

\begin{split}&\bra{\psi}g_{1}\ket{\psi}=(-q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
\implies&\bra{\psi}\left(\tau g_{1}g_{0}\right)^{2\mathrm{R}-2}\ket{\psi}=(-q)^{-1}\bra{\psi}\left(g_{1}g_{2}g_{3}\ldots g_{2\mathrm{R}-2}\right)\left(g_{1}g_{2}\ldots g_{2\mathrm{R}-1}g_{2\mathrm{R}-1}\right)\tau^{-2}\ket{\psi}\\
&=(-q)^{-2}\bra{\psi}\overline{Y}_{A}\tau\overline{Y}_{A}\tau^{-1}\ket{\psi}\,.\end{split}

(347)

The eigenvalue of
\overline{Y}_{A}
is the same as
\tau\overline{Y}_{A}\tau^{-1}
,
46
46

46

To see this note as
\tau
and
\overline{Y}
commute with each other, we can write
\overline{Y}=\tau\overline{Y}_{A}\tau^{-1}+\tau\overline{Y}_{B}\tau^{-1}
, and the eigenvalue of each component can be again found to be the same as before.
therefore the eigenvalue of
\left(\tau g_{1}g_{0}\right)
is given by

\left((-q)^{-2}\exp\left({\rm i}2\theta\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mathrm{R}-2}}=\exp\left(2{\rm i}\left(\theta-\gamma\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2\mathrm{R}-2}}=\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}\,2\pi}{2\mathrm{R}-2}\left(2n\pm\frac{s\mp 1}{p+1}\right)\right)\,.

(348)

Again, like in the case of
(1,2)
defect, the log of the eigenvalues obtained in Eq. (
348
) gives us
h-\bar{h}
, where
h
and
\bar{h}
are the conformal dimension appearing in the
(1,3)
twisted partition function. But in this case, from Eq. (
348
) we see that the length of the spin chain must be taken to be
2\mathrm{R}-2
in the finite size scaling analysis of the momentum to get exact values, as we had noted in section
8
.
